We just did this huge campaign launch last week and the venue was absolutely gorgeous, right on the water, they had this amazing string quartet playing... but yeah, I'll go into the kitchen for just a taste of something, and then suddenly I realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, the leftover dessert from a client dinner, and whatever snacks were sitting in the pantry, all in one sitting. I just don't know how it happens so fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It’s... I mean, afterward, I just feel so incredibly embarrassed and, honestly, disgusted with myself. It’s like I’ve completely blown this whole disciplined person I try so hard to be. I’ll just sit there in the dark kitchen thinking, you know, I should have gone for a run instead, or done some yoga. \n\nI actually used to go to this really great vinyasa flow class on Tuesdays to unwind. The instructor, Maya, she had the most soothing voice, and she’d always play this indie-acoustic playlist that was just perfect for winding down. I haven't been in months, though, because the parking around that studio is an absolute nightmare since they started that water main construction on 4th street. They’ve had the road blocked off since, what, April? It takes twenty minutes just to go two blocks, and the detour signs make absolutely no sense. I even tried taking an Uber once, but the driver got completely turned around... \n\nBut, um, anyway, yes. Sitting there after eating all that food, I just feel terrible. I just feel so ashamed that I couldn't keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. Once I start, especially if it's been a tense night with my husband—which, honestly, is happening more often lately because we're trying to figure out if we should renovate the kitchen or just move. He wants to move closer to the city, but the interest rates right now are just ridiculous. My friend Sarah just bought a place in the suburbs and she's paying almost double what we pay for our mortgage, and her yard isn't even fenced in. She has this golden retriever, Bailey, who is the sweetest dog, but he keeps running into the neighbor's yard and digging up their hydrangeas. \n\nBut, um, anyway, what I mean is, when I start eating, it feels like the decision-making part of me just... goes offline. I'll just keep reaching for more food, even while I'm sitting there thinking to myself, \"Patricia, stop, this is enough.\" It's like I'm watching myself do it but I can't actually make my hands stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. Like I was just saying, it's like I have zero control. Once I start, especially if we've been arguing about the house—which reminds me, the contractor we talked to last week, Dave, he was supposed to send over the estimates for just redoing the cabinets instead of a full gut job. He said he'd have it to me by Tuesday, and it's Thursday now. It's so frustrating trying to get people to just do what they say they're going to do. I mean, at my job, if I missed a deadline by two days, I'd be dealing with some very angry clients. We had this one client last year who threatened to pull their entire account because a proof was four hours late... \n\nBut yeah, when I'm in the kitchen, it's like my brain just disconnects. I have no control over what I'm grabbing or how much. I'll literally be telling myself, \"Patricia, stop, this is enough,\" but my hands just keep going. It's like I'm on autopilot.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the evidence of your late-night eating from your husband or others in the household?\n\nPatient: Hide the evidence? Um, I don't really think so. I usually just toss the wrappers or empty containers right into the regular kitchen trash. Which, actually, reminds me, I really need to buy more of those heavy-duty trash bags. We switched to the generic store brand last month because they were on sale in bulk, and let me tell you, they are absolutely terrible. The bottom ripped out completely when my husband was taking it out to the curb on Tuesday. \n\nTuesday is our new trash day, by the way, because the city completely redrew the sanitation routes. They sent out this incredibly confusing mailer about it a few weeks ago. As a marketing manager, I just cringed looking at the layout—the font was way too small, the color contrast was awful, and the main message was completely buried on the back page. If someone on my team submitted a draft like that, I'd make them start over from scratch. You just can't expect people to read a wall of text, you know? People's attention spans are so short these days with TikTok and everything. My niece spends hours on that app, just scrolling and scrolling... \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, hiding the food. No, I don't really try to bury the wrappers or sneak them out to the outside bins or anything like that. They just go in the trash.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. I eat incredibly fast. I'll usually just be standing right there at the kitchen island, or I'll be scrolling through work emails on my phone while I do it. Speaking of work emails, they just updated our entire Outlook system at the office over the weekend, and it is a complete disaster. Half my folders didn't migrate over, and IT is completely backed up. I submitted a ticket on Monday, and the guy—Kevin, I think his name is—said it's a known issue with the new server. But it's driving me crazy because I had all my client approvals organized by quarter, and now everything from Q3 is just dumped into my main inbox. It took me an hour yesterday just to find a simple PDF proof for a brochure we're doing for a local dental chain... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'm just scrolling through all that mess while I eat, and I go so fast that I barely even taste the food. Half the time, I just look down and the containers are completely empty before I've really even registered what I was eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel ashamed or guilty specifically because you feel like you've lost control while eating, rather than just because of the amount of food you've consumed?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I mean, I feel guilty about all of it, but yeah, it's definitely the control thing. It's just so humiliating to know that I'm a grown woman and I can't even stop myself from eating a sleeve of crackers. I'm usually so organized, you know? Like with my calendar, I have everything color-coded. Work meetings are blue, personal stuff is green, and the house renovations are orange. Although I'm thinking about changing the renovation color to red because it's just becoming an emergency at this point. \n\nWe went to the tile showroom on Saturday, and the salesperson, Sarah, she was so pushy. She kept trying to upsell us on this imported Italian porcelain that was literally three times our budget. I told my husband, I said, \"I know marketing tactics when I see them,\" and she was definitely working on commission. We ended up just leaving because she wouldn't even show us the basic ceramic subway tile we came in for. Which reminds me, we still need to pick a grout color, and there are like fifty shades of gray... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, about feeling guilty. Yeah, I don't know, I just feel terrible afterward. It's not just the calories or whatever, it's that feeling of just... failing, I guess. Like I should be able to just stop, and I can't.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of guilt you experience after these episodes more because you feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I just answered that, right? I was just saying it's the control thing. Like I said, it's just really humiliating to feel like I'm failing at something so basic. Which is funny because at work, I'm literally the one everyone comes to when they need a project rescued. Just this morning, my boss pulled me into a meeting because the junior team completely botched the rollout for a new social media campaign. They forgot to A/B test the landing page, and the bounce rate was through the roof. I had to spend three hours pulling analytics and re-writing the ad copy myself. \n\nAnd don't even get me started on the graphics they used. It was like they just grabbed the first stock photo they saw without even checking the resolution. It looked so pixelated on mobile. I swear, they don't teach attention to detail in college anymore. When I was getting my master's, my professors would have failed me if I turned in something that sloppy. We had this one professor, Dr. Aris, who would dock a full letter grade if your margins weren't exactly one inch...\n\nBut yeah, like I said, it's the control. I feel guilty because I just can't seem to stop myself when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this after a stressful or upsetting event during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. Stress is definitely a big trigger for it. Like, for the last three months, there's been at least one night almost every week where I end up eating like this after an argument or a late work event, and some weeks it happens twice. \n\nActually, speaking of late work events, we had this massive product launch last Thursday, and they catered it with this local place that was supposed to be amazing. But the logistics were a total nightmare. The delivery guy got lost in the business park, and by the time the food arrived, all the hot appetizers were freezing cold. And of course, the event coordinator—who is new, by the way, and I don't think she's going to last—forgot to order vegetarian options for the VIP clients. I had to literally run across the street in my heels to a deli to grab some salads before the keynote speech started. I blistered my heel so badly, I had to wear flats on Friday, which I hate doing because it totally throws off the hemline of my trousers. Just one or two nights a week, I just... let go, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these late-night episodes feeling like you're doing something secretive?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if \"secretive\" is the right word. I guess I'm usually alone when it happens, mostly because my husband goes to bed early. He's one of those people who can just fall asleep the second his head hits the pillow. It's actually infuriating. I'll be lying there, wide awake, thinking about the quarterly budget projections or whether we picked the right shade of white for the kitchen cabinets. There's \"Alabaster\" and \"Snowbound,\" and you wouldn't think it matters, but in the morning light, \"Alabaster\" pulls a little yellow, and I really don't want a yellow kitchen. I want it crisp, you know? Like a high-end bakery. \n\nI actually went to this amazing bakery in Paris a few years ago for our anniversary. They had these croissants that were so flaky, they just shattered when you bit into them. I tried to recreate them at home once, but laminated dough is an absolute nightmare to work with. You have to keep the butter at the exact right temperature, and our old fridge—which is why we're renovating in the first place—had this terrible temperature fluctuation issue. I lost three batches of dough before I just gave up and bought the frozen ones from Trader Joe's. Which, honestly, are surprisingly good for frozen pastries. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, secretive. I don't know, I just... I'm usually just by myself in the kitchen late at night. I don't really think about it as keeping a secret, it's just when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating at night because you're trying to comfort yourself or deal with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it comfort, exactly. It's more like... I just need to turn my brain off, you know? Like I'm just trying to make everything stop spinning for a minute. Especially when I've been dealing with everyone else's problems all day. Like yesterday, my sister called me in tears because her contractor flaked on her bathroom remodel. And I get it, it's stressful, but she always expects me to fix it. Half the reviews are clearly fake, or they're from people who complain about the most ridiculous things. It's supposedly their most popular one, but honestly, I think it's a little overpowering. I prefer something more subtle, like a clean linen or a light lavender. Lavender is supposed to be good for anxiety, actually. My massage therapist always uses a lavender oil when I go in for my monthly deep tissue. She's amazing, she used to work for a professional ballet company, so she really knows how to work out the knots. \n\nBut, um, what were you saying about anxiety? I don't really know if the eating helps with that. I haven't really noticed it making me feel better, it's just something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating at night makes you feel better emotionally after a stressful day?\n\nPatient: Better? No. God, no. If anything, it makes it worse. Afterward, I just feel... embarrassed, honestly. And disgusted with myself. Like I've completely blown this whole disciplined person I try so hard to be all day. I'll just sit there at the kitchen island, staring at the empty wrappers, thinking I should have just gone for a run or done yoga instead. \n\nI used to be really into Bikram yoga, actually. Back before I got this promotion at the firm. The studio was right down the street from my old apartment, and the instructor, Marcus, was just phenomenal. He had this very soothing voice, almost like a late-night radio DJ, but he pushed you so hard. The room was heated to exactly 105 degrees, and the humidity... oh, it was intense. You'd sweat out literally everything. I bought this special non-slip mat from Lululemon just for that class because regular mats would turn into a slip-and-slide after about twenty minutes. Lululemon's quality has really gone downhill lately, though, hasn't it? I bought a pair of their Align leggings last month, and they started pilling after just two washes. Two! For a hundred and twenty dollars, you'd think they could figure out how to make fabric that doesn't pill. I tried taking them back, but the girl at the register gave me this whole attitude about their return policy. \n\nAnyway, yeah. I don't feel better after. I just feel like a failure for not doing something productive instead.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if \"cope\" is the right word. I mean, I guess it's a reaction to everything, but I wouldn't say it's an active choice to deal with things. It's more like a reflex. Like, I get home, I'm drained from managing the team, and I just start opening cabinets. It's almost automatic, like how you drive home from work and don't remember the commute. \n\nI actually read this fascinating article the other day about highway hypnosis. It was in that magazine... oh, what's it called... The Atlantic, maybe? No, it was a link someone posted on LinkedIn. Anyway, it was talking about how our brains just switch to autopilot during routine tasks. It's wild to think about. Like, you're operating a two-ton machine at sixty miles an hour, and your brain is just checked out, thinking about what to make for dinner or replaying an argument you had three years ago. \n\nI had this one argument with my college roommate, Sarah, over a stolen yogurt. It's so stupid now, but at the time, it felt like the ultimate betrayal. She ate my last Greek yogurt—the cherry kind with the fruit on the bottom—and then tried to blame it on her boyfriend, who didn't even like yogurt! He was lactose intolerant. He used to drink that oat milk before oat milk was even a trend. Now you can't go to a coffee shop without them asking if you want oat milk. I tried it in a latte once, and it just tasted like soggy cereal to me. I don't get the hype. \n\nBut, uh, coping... I don't know. I just don't think about it as coping. It's just something that happens when I'm empty at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself eating like this at night?\n\nPatient: Well, for the last three months or so, it's been happening at least once a week, usually after something stressful like an argument with my husband or a late event for work. And some weeks it's twice. \n\nSpeaking of work events, we just had this massive launch party for a new client last Thursday, and the catering was just... abysmal. And the wine they served! It was this organic, biodynamic Pinot Grigio that honestly tasted like watered-down apple cider vinegar. I don't understand this obsession with natural wines. My sister-in-law is super into them right now. She brought a bottle to Thanksgiving last year, and it had sediment floating in it. She claimed it was \"texture,\" but it just looked dirty. Thanksgiving was a disaster anyway because the turkey was so dry. We tried to deep-fry it, which you see all over the Food Network, but we didn't calculate the oil displacement correctly, and it almost overflowed onto the patio. \n\nBut yeah, I'd say it's about once or twice a week that I end up doing this at night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, I'm not really sure if it's tied to feeling anxious or overwhelmed. I haven't really paid attention to exactly what mood I'm in right before it happens. It's just... I'm tired, you know? 

Speaking of being tired, I've been trying to find a new mattress because my husband insists our current one is giving him back pain. We went to one of those fancy mattress stores over the weekend, the ones where they make you lie on this diagnostic bed that's supposed to map your pressure points. It felt like a gimmick to me. The salesman—his name was Todd, and he had way too much cologne on, like that old Abercrombie scent from the early 2000s—kept trying to upsell us on this adjustable base that massages you. Who needs their bed to vibrate? It just felt weird, like being in a cheap motel. We ended up leaving without buying anything because Todd wouldn't take no for an answer about the vibrating base. We might just order one of those bed-in-a-box things online. My coworker Jessica got one from Casper, or maybe it was Purple? The one with the weird grid stuff inside. She says she loves it, but she also said she loved that new sci-fi movie that came out last month, and I thought the plot was completely nonsensical. The aliens didn't even have a clear motive for invading. 

But anyway, yeah, I haven't really noticed if I'm upset or anxious before I eat. I just know it happens.

Thanksgiving was a disaster anyway because the turkey was so dry. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I really do. It's like... once I start, especially if my husband and I just had a tense evening, it feels like the decision-making part of me just completely goes offline. I'll just keep reaching for more food, and in my head, I'm literally telling myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough, but my hands just keep going. 

It's usually after we bicker about something stupid, like what to watch on TV. We have like six different streaming services now, which is absurd. Netflix, Hulu, Max, Apple TV... I swear it costs more than cable used to! And he spends forty-five minutes just scrolling through menus. By the time he actually picks a movie, I'm already falling asleep on the couch. We tried to watch that new historical drama last night, the one set in the 1800s. The costumes were gorgeous, but the lighting was so dark you couldn't even see what was happening. Why do directors do that now? They shoot everything in pitch black and call it moody or cinematic. You basically need night vision goggles to watch television these days. I had to go into the menus to adjust the brightness on our TV, and then I accidentally messed up the color settings, so everyone looked slightly orange for the rest of the night. He was so annoyed about it. 

But, um... yeah, once I start eating on those nights, I just can't seem to make myself stop, no matter how hard my brain is telling me to quit.

The salesman—his name was Todd, and he had way too much cologne on, like that old Abercrombie scent from the early 2000s—kept trying to upsell us on this adjustable base that massages you. I honestly don't know. I've never really thought about whether I learned to do it or if it just sort of crept up on me. It's hard to pinpoint when habits actually start, you know? 

Speaking of learning things to deal with stress, my friend Susan told me I should take up a hobby to unwind, so I tried to learn how to knit a few weeks ago. Do you have any idea how much an emergency vet visit costs these days? It's absolute extortion. They charged us five hundred dollars just to induce vomiting. But um, anyway, to answer your question... no, I really have no idea how the eating at night started. I just don't know.

Yeah, I... I think I already mentioned this, but yes, definitely. It really feels like I just lose all control. Once I start, especially if my husband and I had a tense evening—which, let's be honest, is happening more and more lately—it's like the decision-making part of my brain just totally goes offline. I'll just keep reaching for more food, and in my head, I'm literally telling myself, *Patricia, stop, this is enough*, but my hands just keep going. 

It's so frustrating, you know? Like when you know you shouldn't do something but you do it anyway. Kind of like how I keep going back to that same hair salon downtown even though the stylist, Brenda, never listens to what I want. Last time, I asked for just a trim, maybe a half-inch off the ends because I'm trying to grow it out for my sister's wedding next spring. She's getting married in this beautiful botanical garden, assuming it doesn't rain, which it always does in April. Anyway, Brenda just started chopping away while she was telling me this incredibly long story about her daughter's disastrous middle school band concert. Apparently, half the kids forgot their sheet music. But by the time she finished the story, she had taken off at least three inches! I was furious, but I'm terrible at confrontation, so I just paid her, left a tip—why do we tip people who ruin our hair?—and cried in my car. I had to go buy expensive thickening spray just to make it look decent for work the next day. 

But um, yeah, back to the food... it's exactly like that. I just can't seem to make myself stop once I start, no matter what my brain is screaming at me.

Which is funny because at work, I'm literally the one everyone comes to when they need a project rescued. Just this morning, my boss pulled me into a meeting because the junior team completely botched the rollout for a new social media campaign. I feel guilty because I just can't seem to stop myself when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this after a stressful or upsetting event during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. Like, for the last three months, there's been at least one night almost every week where I end up eating like this after an argument or a late work event, and some weeks it happens twice. \n\nActually, speaking of late work events, we had this massive product launch last Thursday, and they catered it with this local place that was supposed to be amazing. I guess I'm usually alone when it happens, mostly because my husband goes to bed early. He's one of those people who can just fall asleep the second his head hits the pillow. I want it crisp, you know? Like a high-end bakery. \n\nI actually went to this amazing bakery in Paris a few years ago for our anniversary. They had these croissants that were so flaky, they just shattered when you bit into them. I tried to recreate them at home once, but laminated dough is an absolute nightmare to work with. You have to keep the butter at the exact right temperature, and our old fridge—which is why we're renovating in the first place—had this terrible temperature fluctuation issue. I lost three batches of dough before I just gave up and bought the frozen ones from Trader Joe's. Which, honestly, are surprisingly good for frozen pastries. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, secretive. I don't know, I just... I'm usually just by myself in the kitchen late at night. I don't really think about it as keeping a secret, it's just when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating at night because you're trying to comfort yourself or deal with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it comfort, exactly. It's more like... I just need to turn my brain off, you know? Like I'm just trying to make everything stop spinning for a minute. Especially when I've been dealing with everyone else's problems all day. Like yesterday, my sister called me in tears because her contractor flaked on her bathroom remodel. And I get it, it's stressful, but she always expects me to fix it. I ended up spending my entire lunch hour calling plumbers in her area, comparing quotes, checking Yelp reviews. \n\nAnd Yelp is just useless now, isn't it? Half the reviews are clearly fake, or they're from people who complain about the most ridiculous things. I saw one review for a really high-end sushi place we love downtown—the one with the amazing omakase menu—and this woman gave them one star because the lighting was too dim for her to take good Instagram photos of her food. Can you believe that? The chef there trained in Tokyo for ten years, and she's worried about the lighting. We actually went there for my husband's promotion dinner last month, and the yellowtail was just incredible. It practically melted. Though getting a reservation is impossible unless you know the maitre d', which thankfully, my husband does because they play golf together. \n\nBut yeah, the emotions... I guess I'm not really thinking about comforting myself. I just don't really know why I do it, it just happens when I'm overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating at night helps you deal with feelings of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it helps, to be honest. I don't think I've ever really stopped to analyze it like that. I just know that when I'm stressed, especially about the renovation... like, right now we're trying to pick out the backsplash for the kitchen, and my husband wants this dark, moody subway tile, but I keep telling him it's going to make the space look so small. We don't have a lot of natural light in there to begin with because of the oak tree in the neighbor's yard. \n\nSpeaking of that tree, I swear the roots are starting to mess with our plumbing. The guest bathroom sink has been draining so slowly lately, and I tried using that industrial drain cleaner, but the smell was just horrific. It smelled like rotten eggs and chemicals, and it lingered for days. I had to buy three of those expensive soy candles from Anthropologie just to mask it. The \"Volcano\" scent, you know? It's supposedly their most popular one, but honestly, I think it's a little overpowering. I prefer something more subtle, like a clean linen or a light lavender. Lavender is supposed to be good for anxiety, actually. My massage therapist always uses a lavender oil when I go in for my monthly deep tissue. She's amazing, she used to work for a professional ballet company, so she really knows how to work out the knots. \n\nBut, um, what were you saying about anxiety? I don't really know if the eating helps with that. I haven't really noticed it making me feel better, it's just something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating at night makes you feel better emotionally after a stressful day?\n\nPatient: Better? No. God, no. If anything, it makes it worse. Afterward, I just feel... embarrassed, honestly. And disgusted with myself. Like I've completely blown this whole disciplined person I try so hard to be all day. I'll just sit there at the kitchen island, staring at the empty wrappers, thinking I should have just gone for a run or done yoga instead. \n\nI used to be really into Bikram yoga, actually. Back before I got this promotion at the firm. The studio was right down the street from my old apartment, and the instructor, Marcus, was just phenomenal. He had this very soothing voice, almost like a late-night radio DJ, but he pushed you so hard. The room was heated to exactly 105 degrees, and the humidity... oh, it was intense. You'd sweat out literally everything. I bought this special non-slip mat from Lululemon just for that class because regular mats would turn into a slip-and-slide after about twenty minutes. Lululemon's quality has really gone downhill lately, though, hasn't it? I bought a pair of their Align leggings last month, and they started pilling after just two washes. Two! For a hundred and twenty dollars, you'd think they could figure out how to make fabric that doesn't pill. I tried taking them back, but the girl at the register gave me this whole attitude about their return policy. \n\nAnyway, yeah. I don't feel better after. I just feel like a failure for not doing something productive instead.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if \"cope\" is the right word. I mean, I guess it's a reaction to everything, but I wouldn't say it's an active choice to deal with things. It's more like a reflex. Like, I get home, I'm drained from managing the team, and I just start opening cabinets. It's almost automatic, like how you drive home from work and don't remember the commute. \n\nI actually read this fascinating article the other day about highway hypnosis. It was in that magazine... oh, what's it called... The Atlantic, maybe? No, it was a link someone posted on LinkedIn. Anyway, it was talking about how our brains just switch to autopilot during routine tasks. It's wild to think about. Like, you're operating a two-ton machine at sixty miles an hour, and your brain is just checked out, thinking about what to make for dinner or replaying an argument you had three years ago. \n\nI had this one argument with my college roommate, Sarah, over a stolen yogurt. It's so stupid now, but at the time, it felt like the ultimate betrayal. She ate my last Greek yogurt—the cherry kind with the fruit on the bottom—and then tried to blame it on her boyfriend, who didn't even like yogurt! He was lactose intolerant. He used to drink that oat milk before oat milk was even a trend. Now you can't go to a coffee shop without them asking if you want oat milk. I tried it in a latte once, and it just tasted like soggy cereal to me. I don't get the hype. \n\nBut, uh, coping... I don't know. I just don't think about it as coping. It's just something that happens when I'm empty at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself eating like this at night?\n\nPatient: Well, for the last three months or so, it's been happening at least once a week, usually after something stressful like an argument with my husband or a late event for work. And some weeks it's twice. \n\nSpeaking of work events, we just had this massive launch party for a new client last Thursday, and the catering was just... abysmal. They hired this trendy new company that specializes in \"deconstructed\" appetizers. I mean, what is the point of a deconstructed bruschetta? You just end up with a plate of dry toast and a little pile of tomatoes that you have to assemble yourself while trying to hold a glass of wine and make small talk. It's ridiculous. And the wine they served! It was this organic, biodynamic Pinot Grigio that honestly tasted like watered-down apple cider vinegar. I don't understand this obsession with natural wines. My sister-in-law is super into them right now. She brought a bottle to Thanksgiving last year, and it had sediment floating in it. She claimed it was \"texture,\" but it just looked dirty. Thanksgiving was a disaster anyway because the turkey was so dry. We tried to deep-fry it, which you see all over the Food Network, but we didn't calculate the oil displacement correctly, and it almost overflowed onto the patio. \n\nBut yeah, I'd say it's about once or twice a week that I end up doing this at night.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating at night in response to feeling anxious, upset, or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm not really sure if it's tied to feeling anxious or overwhelmed. I haven't really paid attention to exactly what mood I'm in right before it happens. It's just... I'm tired, you know? \n\nSpeaking of being tired, I've been trying to find a new mattress because my husband insists our current one is giving him back pain. We went to one of those fancy mattress stores over the weekend, the ones where they make you lie on this diagnostic bed that's supposed to map your pressure points. It felt like a gimmick to me. The salesman—his name was Todd, and he had way too much cologne on, like that old Abercrombie scent from the early 2000s—kept trying to upsell us on this adjustable base that massages you. Who needs their bed to vibrate? It just felt weird, like being in a cheap motel. We ended up leaving without buying anything because Todd wouldn't take no for an answer about the vibrating base. We might just order one of those bed-in-a-box things online. My coworker Jessica got one from Casper, or maybe it was Purple? The one with the weird grid stuff inside. She says she loves it, but she also said she loved that new sci-fi movie that came out last month, and I thought the plot was completely nonsensical. The aliens didn't even have a clear motive for invading. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I haven't really noticed if I'm upset or anxious before I eat. I just know it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you eat once you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I really do. It's like... once I start, especially if my husband and I just had a tense evening, it feels like the decision-making part of me just completely goes offline. I'll just keep reaching for more food, and in my head, I'm literally telling myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough, but my hands just keep going. \n\nIt's usually after we bicker about something stupid, like what to watch on TV. We have like six different streaming services now, which is absurd. Netflix, Hulu, Max, Apple TV... I swear it costs more than cable used to! And he spends forty-five minutes just scrolling through menus. By the time he actually picks a movie, I'm already falling asleep on the couch. We tried to watch that new historical drama last night, the one set in the 1800s. The costumes were gorgeous, but the lighting was so dark you couldn't even see what was happening. Why do directors do that now? They shoot everything in pitch black and call it moody or cinematic. You basically need night vision goggles to watch television these days. I had to go into the menus to adjust the brightness on our TV, and then I accidentally messed up the color settings, so everyone looked slightly orange for the rest of the night. He was so annoyed about it. \n\nBut, um... yeah, once I start eating on those nights, I just can't seem to make myself stop, no matter how hard my brain is telling me to quit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you learned to eat at night as a way to deal with stress or if it just started happening on its own?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never really thought about whether I learned to do it or if it just sort of crept up on me. It's hard to pinpoint when habits actually start, you know? \n\nSpeaking of learning things to deal with stress, my friend Susan told me I should take up a hobby to unwind, so I tried to learn how to knit a few weeks ago. I went to this little boutique yarn shop downtown, the kind that smells heavily of lavender and only plays acoustic guitar covers of 80s pop songs. The woman working there was incredibly condescending. I asked for just basic starter yarn, and she looked at me like I was an absolute peasant and insisted I needed this hand-dyed, ethically sourced alpaca wool. It was like thirty dollars a skein! And it turns out I'm completely allergic to alpaca. I spent three nights sneezing my head off trying to knit a simple scarf, and because I didn't know what I was doing, it ended up looking like a weird, lumpy triangle. \n\nI actually gave it to our golden retriever, Buster, to play with, but then he swallowed a huge chunk of it. We had to rush him to the emergency vet at two in the morning. Do you have any idea how much an emergency vet visit costs these days? It's absolute extortion. They charged us five hundred dollars just to induce vomiting. And the waiting room was freezing, plus the vending machine was broken so I couldn't even get a bottle of water while we waited for two hours. \n\nBut um, anyway, to answer your question... no, I really have no idea how the eating at night started. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating once you start during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I think I already mentioned this, but yes, definitely. It really feels like I just lose all control. Once I start, especially if my husband and I had a tense evening—which, let's be honest, is happening more and more lately—it's like the decision-making part of my brain just totally goes offline. I'll just keep reaching for more food, and in my head, I'm literally telling myself, *Patricia, stop, this is enough*, but my hands just keep going. \n\nIt's so frustrating, you know? Like when you know you shouldn't do something but you do it anyway. Kind of like how I keep going back to that same hair salon downtown even though the stylist, Brenda, never listens to what I want. Last time, I asked for just a trim, maybe a half-inch off the ends because I'm trying to grow it out for my sister's wedding next spring. She's getting married in this beautiful botanical garden, assuming it doesn't rain, which it always does in April. Anyway, Brenda just started chopping away while she was telling me this incredibly long story about her daughter's disastrous middle school band concert. Apparently, half the kids forgot their sheet music. But by the time she finished the story, she had taken off at least three inches! I was furious, but I'm terrible at confrontation, so I just paid her, left a tip—why do we tip people who ruin our hair?—and cried in my car. I had to go buy expensive thickening spray just to make it look decent for work the next day. \n\nBut um, yeah, back to the food... it's exactly like that. I just can't seem to make myself stop once I start, no matter what my brain is screaming at me."
